=== PROMPT VARIANT ===
r1

=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
For a binary string B = B_1 B_2 \dots B_{3^n} of length 3^n (n \geq 1), we define an operation to obtain a binary string C = C_1 C_2 \dots C_{3^{n-1}} of length 3^{n-1} as follows:

- Partition the elements of B into groups of 3 and take the majority value from each group. That is, for i=1,2,\dots,3^{n-1}, let C_i be the value that appears most frequently among B_{3i-2}, B_{3i-1}, and B_{3i}.

You are given a binary string A = A_1 A_2 \dots A_{3^N} of length 3^N. Let A' = A'_1 be the length-1 string obtained by applying the above operation N times to A.
Determine the minimum number of elements of A that must be changed (from 0 to 1 or from 1 to 0) in order to change the value of A'_1.

Input

The input is given from Standard Input in the following format:
N
A_1 A_2 \dots A_{3^N}

Output

Print the answer.

Constraints


- N is an integer with 1 \leq N \leq 13.
- A is a string of length 3^N consisting of 0 and 1.

Sample Input 1

2
010011101

Sample Output 1

1

For example, with A=010011101, after applying the operation twice, we obtain:

- First operation: The majority of 010 is 0, of 011 is 1, and of 101 is 1, resulting in 011.
- Second operation: The majority of 011 is 1, yielding 1.

To change the final value from 1 to 0, one way is to change the 5th character of A from 1 to 0, yielding A=010001101. After the change, the operations yield:

- First operation: The majority of 010 is 0, of 001 is 0, and of 101 is 1, resulting in 001.
- Second operation: The majority of 001 is 0, yielding 0.

Thus, the minimum number of changes required is 1.

Sample Input 2

1
000

Sample Output 2

2

```

Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Try to avoid patterns like these:
- spending too long comparing nearby plans before committing to one workable approach;
- returning from code to broad algorithm discussion without a concrete contradiction;
- turning one local doubt into a full restart when the current draft only needs a small patch.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

Need compute minimal number of leaf flips to change root value in complete ternary tree of height N where each internal node is majority of 3 children. Need min leaf changes to flip root output. Since majority of 3 children: output 1 iff at least 2 children are 1. To flip root, need change enough leaf bits. Need compute "sensitivity" or minimum weight of "certificate" maybe. For monotone Boolean function majority-of-3 tree. Need min number of leaf flips to change output. This is "minimum distance to opposite constant?" Actually from given assignment A, min Hamming distance to another assignment with root value opposite. This is "minimum flip cost" to change output. Since function monotone, if root=1, need change to 0; by flipping 1s to 0s only? Is it ever beneficial to flip 0 to1? No, to decrease majority output from 1 to0, flipping 0 to1 cannot help, only hurts. Similarly if root=0, flip 0s to1s. So min changes is min number of 1 leaves to remove to make root 0, or min number of 0 leaves to add to make root 1.

But there may be a simpler formula for flip cost based on current parent and child flip costs. Let's derive to reduce operations.

Given child current bits b_i and flip costs f_i. We want min cost to make parent target t = 1 - maj(b). Let's enumerate by current parent.

Case 1: current parent = 1. So at least two children are 1. Target = 0. Need after flips at most one child is 1. We can flip some 1s to 0; flipping 0s to 1 cannot help (would increase number of 1s), so optimal flips only children currently 1? Wait if we flip a 0 to 1, number of 1s increases, making target 0 harder, so never beneficial. Thus choose subset of current 1 children to flip to 0. Need resulting number of 1s <=1. Let k = number of current 1 children (2 or 3). Need flip at least k-1 of them. Min cost = sum of (k-1) smallest f_i among current 1 children. If k=2, need flip one of the two 1s: min(f of those). If k=3, need flip two of three 1s: sum of two smallest f among all. Is that all? What if flipping a 0 to1 and more 1s to0? That increases cost, not needed because flipping only 1s can achieve at most one 1. So yes.

Case 2: current parent = 0. At most one child is 1. Target = 1. Need after flips at least two children are 1. Flipping 1s to 0 cannot help; optimal flip only current 0 children to 1. Let z = number of current 0 children (2 or 3). Need flip at least 2 - (# current 1s) of the 0 children to 1. If current has one 1 (z=2), need flip one 0 child to 1: min f among 0 children. If current has zero 1s (z=3), need flip two 0 children to 1: sum of two smallest f among all. Thus formula:
- If cur == 1:
    k = b0+b1+b2 (# ones)
    if k == 2: flip_cost = min(f_i for b_i=1)
    else (k=3): flip_cost = sum of two smallest f_i (all ones)
- If cur == 0:
    z = # zeros = 3-k
    if z == 2 (k=1): flip_cost = min(f_i for b_i=0)
    else (z=3, k=0): flip_cost = sum of two smallest f_i (all zeros)

This is much faster. Need verify with examples.

Need prove formula. For monotone majority, to decrease output, only flip 1s to0; to increase, only flip 0s to1. Since flipping opposite direction can only move output further from target, never reduces number of flips. More formally, any solution flipping some 0 to1 when target lower can remove that flip and still target lower? If target=0 and you flip a 0 to1, it increases ones; removing it decreases ones, so majority remains <=? If after flips majority 0, removing a 0->1 flip (restoring 0) decreases number of ones, so majority still 0. Thus no optimal uses it. Similarly target=1. Good.

Pseudo:

import sys
def main():
    data = sys.stdin.read().split()
    if not data: return
    N = int(data[0])
    A = ''.join(data[1:])
    # maybe if A length less due to newline? okay
    vals = [1 if ch == '1' else 0 for ch in A]
    costs = [1] * len(vals)
    for _ in range(N):
        L = len(vals)
        newL = L // 3
        new_vals = [0] * newL
        new_costs = [0] * newL
        idx = 0
        for i in range(0, L, 3):
            b0 = vals[i]; b1 = vals[i+1]; b2 = vals[i+2]
            f0 = costs[i]; f1 = costs[i+1]; f2 = costs[i+2]
            s = b0 + b1 + b2
            if s >= 2:
                cur = 1
                if s == 2:
                    # two ones; need flip one of them
                    # min f among ones
                    if b0 == 1:
                        fc = f0
                        if b1 == 1:
                            if f1 < fc: fc = f1
                        else: # b2==1
                            if f2 < fc: fc = f2
                    else: # b1==b2==1
                        fc = f1 if f1 < f2 else f2
                else: # s==3
                    # sum two smallest f among f0,f1,f2
                    # compute
                    if f0 <= f1 and f0 <= f2:
                        fc = f0 + (f1 if f1 < f2 else f2)
                    elif f1 <= f0 and f1 <= f2:
                        fc = f1 + (f0 if f0 < f2 else f2)
                    else:
                        fc = f2 + (f0 if f0 < f1 else f1)
            else:
                cur = 0
                if s == 1:
                    # one one, two zeros; need flip one zero to 1
                    # min f among zeros
                    if b0 == 0:
                        fc = f0
                        if b1 == 0:
                            if f1 < fc: fc = f1
                        else: # b2==0
                            if f2 < fc: fc = f2
                    elif b1 == 0: # b0=1, b1=0, b2=0? Actually s=1, if b0=1 then b1=b2=0
                        fc = f1 if f1 < f2 else f2
                    else: # b0=0,b1=0,b2=1
                        fc = f0 if f0 < f1 else f1
                else: # s==0
                    # sum two smallest f
                    if f0 <= f1 and f0 <= f2:
                        fc = f0 + (f1 if f1 < f2 else f2)
                    elif f1 <= f0 and f1 <= f2:
                        fc = f1 + (f0 if f0 < f2 else f2)
                    else:
                        fc = f2 + (f0 if f0 < f1 else f1)
            new_vals[idx] = cur
            new_costs[idx] = fc
            idx += 1
        vals = new_vals
        costs = new_costs
    print(costs[0])  # flip cost of root to opposite

Let's draft code:

import sys

def main():
    data = sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()
    if not data:
        return
    N = int(data[0])
    if len(data) == 2:
        s = data[1]
    else:
        s = b''.join(data[1:])
    vals = [c & 1 for c in s]
    costs = [1] * len(vals)

    for _ in range(N):
        L = len(vals)
        newL = L // 3
        new_vals = [0] * newL
        new_costs = [0] * newL
        idx = 0
        # local variables for speed?
        v = vals
        cst = costs
        for i in range(0, L, 3):
            b0 = v[i]
            b1 = v[i+1]
            b2 = v[i+2]
            f0 = cst[i]
            f1 = cst[i+1]
            f2 = cst[i+2]
            s = b0 + b1 + b2
            if s >= 2:
                cur = 1
                if s == 2:
                    # exactly two ones
                    if b0 == 1:
                        fc = f0
                        if b1 == 1:
                            if f1 < fc:
                                fc = f1
                        else:
                            if f2 < fc:
                                fc = f2
                    else:
                        # b1 and b2 are ones
                        fc = f1 if f1 < f2 else f2
                else:
                    # three ones: sum of two smallest costs
                    # compute two smallest among f0,f1,f2
                    if f0 <= f1 and f0 <= f2:
                        fc = f0 + (f1 if f1 < f2 else f2)
                    elif f1 <= f0 and f1 <= f2:
                        fc = f1 + (f0 if f0 < f2 else f2)
                    else:
                        fc = f2 + (f0 if f0 < f1 else f1)
            else:
                cur = 0
                if s == 1:
                    # exactly one one, two zeros
                    if b0 == 0:
                        fc = f0
                        if b1 == 0:
                            if f1 < fc:
                                fc = f1
                        else:
                            if f2 < fc:
                                fc = f2
                    elif b1 == 0:
                        # b0 is one, b1 and b2 are zeros
                        fc = f1 if f1 < f2 else f2
                    else:
                        # b0 and b1 are zeros, b2 is one
                        fc = f0 if f0 < f1 else f1
                else:
                    # three zeros: sum of two smallest costs
                    if f0 <= f1 and f0 <= f2:
                        fc = f0 + (f1 if f1 < f2 else f2)
                    elif f1 <= f0 and f1 <= f2:
                        fc = f1 + (f0 if f0 < f2 else f2)
                    else:
                        fc = f2 + (f0 if f0 < f1 else f1)
            new_vals[idx] = cur
            new_costs[idx] = fc
            idx += 1
        vals = new_vals
        costs = new_costs

    print(costs[0])

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()

Let's maybe prove formula for fc in code more formally:
Let child outputs b_i, flip costs f_i.
If current majority is 1:
- If exactly two 1s: to make majority 0, at most one 1. Since currently two 1s, must change at least one 1 to 0. Changing one 1 to 0 suffices. Changing any 0 to1 only increases ones, so not useful. Min cost is min f_i among children with b_i=1.
- If three 1s: need at most one 1, so must change at least two 1s to0. Changing two suffices. Min cost is sum of two smallest f_i.
If current majority is 0:
- If exactly one 1: to make majority1, need at least two 1s, so change at least one 0 to1. Min cost among b_i=0.
- If zero 1s: need at least two 1s, so change at least two 0s to1. Min sum of two smallest f_i.
This is exactly code.

Now, let's think about if there is a scenario where current majority is 1 with exactly two 1s, and one of the 1 children has very high flip cost, but flipping the zero child to1 and one 1 child to0 also results in two 1s? Wait target0 requires at most one 1. If we flip zero to1 and one 1 to0, ones count remains 2 (because -1+1), not target. Need flip two 1s to0 if also flip zero. Cost higher. So no.

Current majority 0 with one 1, target1. If flip the one 1 to0 and two zeros to1, ones count becomes2, target. Cost f_one + sum two zeros. Could this be cheaper than flipping one zero? No, because f_one positive, so flipping one zero alone gives ones count2? Wait current [1,0,0], ones=1. Flip one zero to1 => [1,1,0], ones=2, target. Cost min zero. So no need to flip the one. Good.

Let's draft final code:

import sys

def main():
    data = sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()
    if not data:
        return
    N = int(data[0])
    if len(data) == 2:
        s = data[1]
    else:
        s = b''.join(data[1:])

    vals = [c & 1 for c in s]
    costs = [1] * len(vals)

    for _ in range(N):
        L = len(vals)
        newL = L // 3
        new_vals = [0] * newL
        new_costs = [0] * newL
        v = vals
        cst = costs
        nv = new_vals
        nc = new_costs
        idx = 0
        for i in range(0, L, 3):
            b0 = v[i]
            b1 = v[i+1]
            b2 = v[i+2]
            f0 = cst[i]
            f1 = cst[i+1]
            f2 = cst[i+2]

            ones = b0 + b1 + b2
            if ones >= 2:
                cur = 1
                if ones == 2:
                    if b0 == 1:
                        fc = f0
                        if b1 == 1:
                            if f1 < fc:
                                fc = f1
                        else:
                            if f2 < fc:
                                fc = f2
                    else:
                        fc = f1 if f1 < f2 else f2
                else:
                    m = f0 if f0 > f1 else f1
                    if f2 > m:
                        m = f2
                    fc = f0 + f1 + f2 - m
            else:
                cur = 0
                if ones == 1:
                    if b0 == 0:
                        fc = f0
                        if b1 == 0:
                            if f1 < fc:
                                fc = f1
                        else:
                            if f2 < fc:
                                fc = f2
                    elif b1 == 0:
                        fc = f1 if f1 < f2 else f2
                    else:
                        fc = f0 if f0 < f1 else f1
                else:
                    m = f0 if f0 > f1 else f1
                    if f2 > m:
                        m = f2
                    fc = f0 + f1 + f2 - m

            nv[idx] = cur
            nc[idx] = fc
            idx += 1

        vals = new_vals
        costs = new_costs

    print(costs[0])

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()

Potential optimization: Use `for i in range(0, L, 3):` and local variables. We can reduce branch complexity by using formula with `min`/`max` built-ins? Built-ins in C may be faster than Python branches. Let's consider alternative implementation using built-in `min` and `max`:
For each triple:
ones = b0+b1+b2
cur = 1 if ones >=2 else 0
if ones == 2:
    fc = min(f0 if b0 else INF, f1 if b1 else INF, f2 if b2 else INF)
elif ones == 1:
    fc = min(f0 if not b0 else INF, f1 if not b1 else INF, f2 if not b2 else INF)
elif ones == 0 or ones == 3:
    fc = f0 + f1 + f2 - max(f0,f1,f2)
But this uses generator expressions and built-in min/max, maybe slower due to Python generator. Could use conditional expressions in tuple:
fc = min((f0 if b0 else 10**9), (f1 if b1 else 10**9), (f2 if b2 else 10**9))
This creates tuple and calls min C, but tuple creation overhead. For 2.4M, maybe okay but not sure. Branch code likely faster.

Potential optimization: Use `for i in range(0, L, 3):` and local variables. We can reduce branch complexity by using formula with `min`/`max` built-ins? Built-ins in C may be faster than Python branches. Let's consider alternative implementation using built-in `min` and `max`:
For each triple:
ones = b0+b1+b2
cur = 1 if ones >=2 else 0
if ones == 2:
    fc = min(f0 if b0 else INF, f1 if b1 else INF, f2 if b2 else INF)
elif ones == 1:
    fc = min(f0 if not b0 else INF, f1 if not b1 else INF, f2 if not b2 else INF)
elif ones == 0 or ones == 3:
    fc = f0 + f1 + f2 - max(f0,f1,f2)
But this uses generator expressions and built-in min/max, maybe slower due to Python generator. Could use conditional expressions in tuple:
fc = min((f0 if b0 else 10**9), (f1 if b1 else 10**9), (f2 if b2 else 10**9))
This creates tuple and calls min C, but tuple creation overhead. For 2.4M, maybe okay but not sure. Branch code likely faster.

   need flip k = 2-ones zeros to1. Choose k smallest f among zeros.
So general: choose k smallest costs among a subset of children (those with bit equal to cur? Let's see if cur=1, subset = children with b=1, k=ones-1. If cur=0, subset = children with b=0, k=2-ones. Since ones=0 => k=2 among zeros; ones=1 => k=1 among zeros. This is uniform. Could implement by collecting costs of subset into three variables and selecting k smallest. But branch code already.

For each triple:
ones = b0+b1+b2

if ones == 2:
    fc = min(f0 if b0 else INF, f1 if b1 else INF, f2 if b2 else INF)
elif ones == 1:
    fc = min(f0 if not b0 else INF, f1 if not b1 else INF, f2 if not b2 else INF)
elif ones == 0 or ones == 3:
    fc = f0 + f1 + f2 - max(f0,f1,f2)
But this uses generator expressions and built-in min/max, maybe slower due to Python generator. Could use conditional expressions in tuple:
fc = min((f0 if b0 else 10**9), (f1 if b1 else 10**9), (f2 if b2 else 10**9))
This creates tuple and calls min C, but tuple creation overhead. For 2.4M, maybe okay but not sure. Branch code likely faster.

Potential optimization: Use `for i in range(0, L, 3):` and local variables. We can reduce branch complexity by using formula with `min`/`max` built-ins? Built-ins in C may be faster than Python branches. Let's consider alternative implementation using built-in `min` and `max`:
For each triple:
ones = b0+b1+b2
cur = 1 if ones >=2 else 0
if ones == 2:
    fc = min(f0 if b0 else INF, f1 if b1 else INF, f2 if b2 else INF)
elif ones == 1:
    fc = min(f0 if not b0 else INF, f1 if not b1 else INF, f2 if not b2 else INF)
elif ones == 0 or ones == 3:
    fc = f0 + f1 + f2 - max(f0,f1,f2)
But this uses generator expressions and built-in min/max, maybe slower due to Python generator. Could use conditional expressions in tuple:
fc = min((f0 if b0 else 10**9), (f1 if b1 else 10**9), (f2 if b2 else 10**9))
This creates tuple and calls min C, but tuple creation overhead. For 2.4M, maybe okay but not sure. Branch code likely faster.

Potential optimization: Use `for i in range(0, L, 3):` and local variables. We can reduce branch complexity by using formula with `min`/`max` built-ins? Built-ins in C may be faster than Python branches. Let's consider alternative implementation using built-in `min` and `max`:
For each triple:
ones = b0+b1+b2
cur = 1 if ones >=2 else 0
if ones == 2:
    fc = min(f0 if b0 else INF, f1 if b1 else INF, f2 if b2 else INF)
elif ones == 1:
    fc = min(f0 if not b0 else INF, f1 if not b1 else INF, f2 if not b2 else INF)
elif ones == 0 or ones == 3:
    fc = f0 + f1 + f2 - max(f0,f1,f2)
But this uses generator expressions and built-in min/max, maybe slower due to Python generator. Could use conditional expressions in tuple:
fc = min((f0 if b0 else 10**9), (f1 if b1 else 10**9), (f2 if b2 else 10**9))
This creates tuple and calls min C, but tuple creation overhead. For 2.4M, maybe okay but not sure. Branch code likely faster.

   need flip k = 2-ones zeros to1. Choose k smallest f among zeros.
So general: choose k smallest costs among a subset of children (those with bit equal to cur? Let's see if cur=1, subset = children with b=1, k=ones-1. If cur=0, subset = children with b=0, k=2-ones. elif ones == 1:
    fc = min(f0 if not b0 else INF, f1 if not b1 else INF, f2 if not b2 else INF)
elif ones == 0 or ones == 3:
    fc = f0 + f1 + f2 - max(f0,f1,f2)

Potential optimization: Use `for i in range(0, L, 3):` and local variables. We can reduce branch complexity by using formula with `min`/`max` built-ins? Built-ins in C may be faster than Python branches. Let's consider alternative implementation using built-in `min` and `max`:
For each triple:
ones = b0+b1+b2
cur = 1 if ones >=2 else 0
if ones == 2:
    fc = min(f0 if b0 else INF, f1 if b1 else INF, f2 if b2 else INF)
elif ones == 1:
    fc = min(f0 if not b0 else INF, f1 if not b1 else INF, f2 if not b2 else INF)
elif ones == 0 or ones == 3:
    fc = f0 + f1 + f2 - max(f0,f1,f2)
But this uses generator expressions and built-in min/max, maybe slower due to Python generator. Could use conditional expressions in tuple:
fc = min((f0 if b0 else 10**9), (f1 if b1 else 10**9), (f2 if b2 else 10**9))
This creates tuple and calls min C, but tuple creation overhead. For 2.4M, maybe okay but not sure. Branch code likely faster.

Potential optimization: Use `for i in range(0, L, 3):` and local variables. We can reduce branch complexity by using formula with `min`/`max` built-ins? Built-ins in C may be faster than Python branches. Let's consider alternative implementation using built-in `min` and `max`:
For each triple:
ones = b0+b1+b2
cur = 1 if ones >=2 else 0
if ones == 2:
    fc = min(f0 if b0 else INF, f1 if b1 else INF, f2 if b2 else INF)
elif ones == 1:
    fc = min(f0 if not b0 else INF, f1 if not b1 else INF, f2 if not b2 else INF)
elif ones == 0 or ones == 3:
    fc = f0 + f1 + f2 - max(f0,f1,f2)
But this uses generator expressions and built-in min/max, maybe slower due to Python generator. Could use conditional expressions in tuple:
fc = min((f0 if b0 else 10**9), (f1 if b1 else 10**9), (f2 if b2 else 10**9))
This creates tuple and calls min C, but tuple creation overhead. For 2.4M, maybe okay but not sure. Branch code likely faster.

   need flip k = 2-ones zeros to1. Choose k smallest f among zeros.
So general: choose k smallest costs among a subset of children (those with bit equal to cur? Let's see if cur=1, subset = children with b=1, k=ones-1. If cur=0, subset = children with b=0, k=2-ones. elif ones == 0 or ones == 3:

Potential optimization: Use `for i in range(0, L, 3):` and local variables. We can reduce branch complexity by using formula with `min`/`max` built-ins? Built-ins in C may be faster than Python branches. Let's consider alternative implementation using built-in `min` and `max`:
For each triple:
ones = b0+b1+b2
cur = 1 if ones >=2 else 0
if ones == 2:
    fc = min(f0 if b0 else INF, f1 if b1 else INF, f2 if b2 else INF)
elif ones == 1:
    fc = min(f0 if not b0 else INF, f1 if not b1 else INF, f2 if not b2 else INF)
elif ones == 0 or ones == 3:
    fc = f0 + f1 + f2 - max(f0,f1,f2)
But this uses generator expressions and built-in min/max, maybe slower due to Python generator. Could use conditional expressions in tuple:
fc = min((f0 if b0 else 10**9), (f1 if b1 else 10**9), (f2 if b2 else 10**9))
This creates tuple and calls min C, but tuple creation overhead. For 2.4M, maybe okay but not sure. Branch code likely faster.

Potential optimization: Use `for i in range(0, L, 3):` and local variables. We can reduce branch complexity by using formula with `min`/`max` built-ins? Built-ins in C may be faster than Python branches. Let's consider alternative implementation using built-in `min` and `max`:
For each triple:
ones = b0+b1+b2
cur = 1 if ones >=2 else 0
if ones == 2:
    fc = min(f0 if b0 else INF, f1 if b1 else INF, f2 if b2 else INF)
elif ones == 1:
    fc = min(f0 if not b0 else INF, f1 if not b1 else INF, f2 if not b2 else INF)
elif ones == 0 or ones == 3:
    fc = f0 + f1 + f2 - max(f0,f1,f2)
But this uses generator expressions and built-in min/max, maybe slower due to Python generator. Could use conditional expressions in tuple:
fc = min((f0 if b0 else 10**9), (f1 if b1 else 10**9), (f2 if b2 else 10**9))
This creates tuple and calls min C, but tuple creation overhead. For 2.4M, maybe okay but not sure. Branch code likely faster.

   need flip k = 2-ones zeros to1. Choose k smallest f among zeros.
So general: choose k smallest costs among a subset of children (those with bit equal to cur? Let's see if cur=1, subset = children with b=1, k=ones-1. If cur=0, subset = children with b=0, k=2-ones. elif ones == 0 or ones == 3:

Potential optimization: Use `for i in range(0, L, 3):` and local variables. We can reduce branch complexity by using formula with `min`/`max` built-ins? Built-ins in C may be faster than Python branches. Let's consider alternative implementation using built-in `min` and `max`:
For each triple:
ones = b0+b1+b2
cur = 1 if ones >=2 else 0
if ones == 2:
    fc = min(f0 if b0 else INF, f1 if b1 else INF, f2 if b2 else INF)
elif ones == 1:
    fc = min(f0 if not b0 else INF, f1 if not b1 else INF, f2 if not b2 else INF)
elif ones == 0 or ones == 3:
    fc = f0 + f1 + f2 - max(f0,f1,f2)
But this uses generator expressions and built-in min/max, maybe slower due to Python generator. Could use conditional expressions in tuple:
fc = min((f0 if b0 else 10**9), (f1 if b1 else 10**9), (f2 if b2 else 10**9))
This creates tuple and calls min C, but tuple creation overhead. For 2.4M, maybe okay but not sure. Branch code likely faster.
